Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 72 of 103 
projected 30-year Treasury yield for the period 2020-2024 (3.90 percent).79  
1 
In determining the security most relevant to the application of the CAPM, 
2 
it is important to select the term (or maturity) that best matches the life of 
3 
the underlying investment.  As noted by Morningstar: 
4 
The traditional thinking regarding the time horizon of 
5 
the chosen Treasury security is that it should match the 
6 
time horizon of whatever is being valued…  Note that 
7 
the horizon is a function of the investment, not the 
8 
investor.  If an investor plans to hold stock in a company 
9 
for only five years, the yield on a five-year Treasury 
10 
note would not be appropriate since the company will 
11 
continue to exist beyond those five years.80 
12 
Because utility companies represent long-duration investments, it is 
13 
appropriate to use yields on long-term Treasury bonds as the risk-free rate 
14 
component of the CAPM.  In my view, the 30-year Treasury bond is the 
15 
appropriate security for that purpose.  Because the cost of capital is intended 
16 
to be forward-looking, it is appropriate to consider projected measures of 
17 
the market risk premium and interest rates. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Please describe your estimate of the market risk premium used in your 
20 
CAPM. 
21 
A. 
The estimated market risk premium is based on the expected return on the 
22 
S&P 500 Index, less the 30-year Treasury bond yield.  The expected return 
23 
on the S&P 500 Index is calculated using a DCF model for all companies 
24 
in the index based on market capitalization-weighted growth rates and 
25 
                                                 
79 Aspen Publishers, Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, Vol. 37, No. 12 December 1, 2018, 
14.. 
80 Morningstar Inc., Ibbotson SBBI 2013 Valuation Yearbook, at 44.

Page 73 of 103 
dividend yields.  The market risk premium implied by each of the three 
1 
Treasury yields discussed above is used in the CAPM analysis. 
2

6

7

8

Q. 
Did you consider another form of the CAPM in your analysis? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  In prior proceedings, the Commission has relied upon the Zero-Beta 
11 
CAPM (the form of which is sometimes referred to as the “Empirical 
12 
CAPM”84) in estimating the cost of equity.  The Zero-Beta CAPM 
13 
calculates the product of the adjusted Beta coefficient and the market risk 
14 
premium and applies a weight of 75.00 percent to that result.  The model 
15 
then applies a 25.00 percent weight to the market risk premium, without any 
16 
effect from the Beta coefficient.  The results of the two calculations are 
17 
summed, along with the risk-free rate, to produce the Zero-Beta CAPM 
18 
result, as noted in Equation [4] below:   
19 
 
ke = rf + 0.75β(rm – rf) + 0.25(rm – rf)  
[4] 
20 
where: 
21

83 Id.

2006, at 189.

Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 76 of 103 
 
ke = the required market ROE 
1 
 
β = Adjusted Beta coefficient of an individual security 
2 
 
rf = the risk-free rate of return 
3 
 
rm = the required return on the market as a whole 
4 
 
5 
In essence, the Zero-Beta form of the CAPM addresses the tendency of the 
6 
“traditional” CAPM to underestimate the cost of equity for companies with 
7 
low Beta coefficients such as regulated utilities.  In that regard, the Zero-
8 
Beta CAPM is not redundant to the use of adjusted Betas; rather, it 
9 
recognizes the results of academic research indicating that the risk-return 
10 
relationship is different (in essence, flatter) than estimated by the CAPM, 
11 
and that the CAPM underestimates the “alpha,” or the constant return 
12 
term.85   
13 
 
14 
As with the CAPM, my application of the Zero-Beta CAPM uses the 
15 
forward-looking market risk premium estimates, the three yields on 30-year 
16 
Treasury securities noted earlier as the risk-free rate, and the Bloomberg 
17 
and Value Line Beta coefficients.  Exhibit __ (AEB-4) show the results of 
18

Q. 
What was the Commission’s reasoning for developing its weighting of 
1 
the DCF and CAPM methodologies in the RD? 
2 
A. 
At the time of the RD, the Commission did not have a significant amount 
3 
of experience with the CAPM.  The RD noted that the Commission had 
4 
historically used the CAPM as a check on its DCF results, and was 
5 
somewhat undecided as to “how far the Commission should go in elevating 
6 
the status of CAPM.”86   The RD opted for a gradual transition towards the 
7 
CAPM, ultimately settling on a 1/3 weighting, indicating that “proposals 
8 
have simply not shown that the CAPM should be raised all at once to parity 
9 
with the DCF analysis in the setting of returns on equity.”87  To the extent 
10 
that this was a consideration in the RD’s weighting determination, the 
11 
Commission’s 25 years of experience with the CAPM since that time 
12 
provides a sound basis for altering the weighting of the two ROE 
13 
methodologies. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please summarize your conclusion regarding the relative weighting of 
16 
the CAPM and DCF results. 
17 
A. 
While the RD proposed the 2/3 weighting on the DCF, the weightings and 
18 
methodologies used to estimate the ROE were left open for additional 
19 
consideration in future rate proceedings.  Since then, the Commission has 
20 
employed the CAPM as one component of the formula used to develop ROE 
21 
estimates.  There does not appear to be any reason to infer that the 
22 
                                                 
86  RD, at 27. 
87  Id. 


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 79 of 103 
Commission has less confidence in the results of the CAPM than those of 
1 
the DCF.  The conditions that warranted the Commission’s GFP inquiry and 
2 
the subsequent RD in the early 1990s exist again today with DCF results 
3 
considerably lower than those from other models, such as the CAPM, as 
4 
well as returns authorized in other jurisdictions.  Finally, to the extent that 
5 
dividend yields are low relative to historical levels and could increase as 
6 
yields on government bonds rise, the DCF model is likely to underestimate 
7 
the cost of equity.  Therefore, it is reasonable to apply equal weighting to 
8 
the DCF and CAPM methods when determining the ROE for the 
9 
Companies.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Are the assumptions used in the CAPM less reliable than the 
12 
assumptions used in the DCF model? 
13 
A. 
Not necessarily.  As discussed previously, the CAPM relies on a risk-free 
14 
rate, Beta and the MRP.  The risk-free rate is readily observable and can be 
15 
projected for the forward-looking period.  Beta is estimated using the 
16 
historical relationship between the risk of the stock and the overall market.  
17 
Finally, the market risk premium, while not observable, can be estimated 
18 
for the forward-looking period. My testimony discusses how the dividend 
19 
yield has been affected by market conditions and therefore, while this 
20 
assumption may be easy to calculate using historical data, it is not 
21 
representative of forward-looking market conditions.  Therefore, while the 
22 
CAPM is often criticized as relying on unobservable assumptions, currently 
23 


--- Page 82 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 80 of 103 
the dividend yield in the DCF model is not reflective of projected market 
1 
conditions. 
2

VII. 
  REGULATORY AND BUSINESS RISKS 
A. RISK ASSESSMENT 
4 
Q. 
Have you performed an analysis of the level of regulatory support that 
5 
the Companies receive in New York as compared to the proxy group 
6 
companies? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  I conducted an analysis of the regulatory protections that are in place 
8 
for KEDNY and KEDLI compared with those for the operating utility 
9 
companies held by the proxy group companies.  The results of my analysis 
10 
are presented in Exhibit ___ (AEB-7).  Specifically, I examined the 
11 
following factors that affect the business risk of KEDNY and KEDLI and 
12 
the proxy group companies: (1) test year convention; (2) fuel cost recovery; 
13 
(3) revenue decoupling; and (4) capital cost recovery. 
14 
 
15 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (AEB-7), the majority of the operating companies 
16 
(i.e., 36 out of 63) in the proxy groups provide service in jurisdictions that 
17 
allow the use of a fully or partially forecast test year.  All of the operating 
18 
companies held by the proxy groups are allowed to pass through fuel costs 
19 
and purchased power costs directly to customers, so that the utility does not 
20 
incur any risk associated with fuel or purchased power costs.  It is important 
21 
to recognize that fuel and purchased power costs typically account for 50 – 
22 


--- Page 83 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 81 of 103 
60 percent of the total operating costs for a regulated utility.  Like KEDNY 
1 
and KEDLI, 60 percent of the operating utilities held by the proxy group 
2 
(i.e., 38 out of 63) have revenue decoupling mechanisms or weather 
3 
normalization adjustment clauses that allow them to break the link between 
4 
customer usage and revenues.  Finally, approximately 56 percent of the 
5 
operating utilities held by the proxy group (35 out of 63) have capital cost 
6 
recovery mechanisms that allow them to recover capital investments that 
7 
are placed into service between rate cases. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Based on these analyses, what is your conclusion regarding the level of 
10 
regulatory support for KEDNY and KEDLI relative to that of the 
11 
proxy group companies? 
12 
A. 
My conclusion is that KEDNY and KEDLI have comparable regulatory 
13 
protection to the proxy group companies. While the Commission has been 
14 
a leader in implementing mechanisms that reduce the business risk of 
15 
regulated utilities in New York, many other jurisdictions have taken similar 
16 
steps in more recent years.  A November 2015 report published by the 
17 
Edison Electric Institute indicates that more and more jurisdictions have 
18 
moved toward the use of forecast test years since the 2013 survey88; fuel 
19 
                                                 
88  Edison Electric Institute, “Alternative Regulation for Emerging Utility Challenges:  
2015 Update,” prepared by Pacific Economics Group, November 11, 2015, at 32.  (EEI 
report states: “The ranks of US jurisdictions that allow the use of forward test years 
have swollen and now encompasses about half of the total.  Since our 2013 survey, 
electric utilities in Pennsylvania have successfully used FTYs and utilities in Arkansas 
and Indiana have received legislative authorization for their use.  Forward test years 
are the norm in Canadian regulation.”)

--- Page 86 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 84 of 103 
B. REGULATORY ENVIRONMENT 
1 
Q. 
Please explain how the regulatory framework affects investors’ risk 
2 
assessments. 
3 
A. 
The ratemaking process is premised on the principle that, for investors and 
4 
companies to commit the capital needed to provide safe and reliable utility 
5 
services, the subject utility must have the opportunity to recover invested 
6 
capital and the market-required return on such capital.  Regulatory 
7 
commissions recognize that because utility operations are capital intensive, 
8 
regulatory decisions should enable the utility to attract capital at reasonable 
9 
terms, which balance the long-term interests of investors and customers.  In 
10 
that respect, the regulatory framework in which a utility operates is one of 
11 
the most important factors considered in both debt and equity investors’ risk 
12 
assessments.  
13 
 
14 
Because investors have many investment alternatives, even within a given 
15 
market sector, the Companies’ authorized returns must be adequate on a 
16 
relative basis to ensure their ability to attract capital under a variety of 
17 
economic and financial market conditions.  From the perspective of debt 
18 
investors, the authorized return should enable the Companies to generate 
19 
the cash flow needed to meet their near-term financial obligations, make the 
20 
capital investments needed to maintain and expand their systems, and 
21 
maintain sufficient levels of liquidity to fund unexpected events.  This 
22 
financial liquidity must be derived not only from internally-generated funds, 
23 
but also from efficient access to capital markets.     
24 


--- Page 87 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 85 of 103 
 
1 
From the perspective of equity investors, the authorized return must be 
2 
adequate to provide a risk-comparable return on the equity portion of the 
3 
Companies’ capital investments.  Because equity investors are the residual 
4 
claimants on the Companies’ cash flows (that is, debt interest must be paid 
5 
prior to any equity dividends), equity investors are particularly concerned 
6 
with the regulatory framework in which a utility operates and its effect on 
7 
future earnings and cash flows. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please explain how credit rating agencies consider the regulatory 
10 
framework in establishing a company’s credit rating.  
11 
A. 
S&P and Moody’s both consider the overall regulatory framework in 
12 
establishing credit ratings.  As shown in Figure 12, Moody’s establishes 
13 
credit ratings based on four key factors:   
14 
Figure 12: Moody’s Rating Factors 
15 
Factor 
Weighting 
Regulatory Framework 
25% 
Ability to Recover Costs and Earn Returns 
25% 
Diversification 
10% 
Financial Strength 
40% 
 
Total 
100% 
 
16 
Two of these factors (i.e., regulatory framework and the ability to recover 
17 
costs and earn returns) are based on the regulatory environment such that 
18 


--- Page 88 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 86 of 103 
half of Moody’s overall assessment of business and financial risk for 
1 
regulated utilities is based upon the regulatory environment.93  Moody’s 
2 
further subdivides the first two factors, regulatory framework and the ability 
3 
to recover costs and earn authorized returns, into sub-factors to help 
4 
“provide more granularity and transparency on the overall regulatory 
5 
environment, which is the most important consideration for this sector.”94 
6 
 
7 
With respect to the regulatory framework, Moody’s looks for transparency, 
8 
predictability, and supportiveness of regulatory commissions.95  For the 
9 
second factor, ability to recover costs and earn returns, Moody’s evaluates 
10 
the regulatory elements that directly affect the ability of the utility to 
11 
generate cash flow and service its debt over time.96  Moody’s views the 
12 
ability to recover costs on a timely basis and to attract debt and equity 
13 
capital as crucial credit considerations noting that “[t]he inability to recover 
14 
costs…has been one of the greatest drivers of financial stress in this 
15 
sector.”97  This is particularly true as utilities are often cash flow negative 
16 
due to large capital expenditures, so any lack of timely recovery or 
17 
sufficiency of rates can strain access to capital markets. 
18 
 
19 
S&P has also identified the regulatory environment as an important factor, 
20 
                                                 
93 Moody’s Investor Service, Rating Methodology, Regulated Electric and Gas Utilities, 
December 23, 2013, at 6. 
94 Id. at 3.  
95 Id. at 9-10. 
96 Id. at 15. 
97 Id. 


--- Page 89 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 87 of 103 
stating, “we believe the fundamental regulatory environment in the 
1 
jurisdictions in which a utility operates often influence credit quality the 
2 
most.”98 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How does the regulatory environment in which a utility operates affect 
5 
its access to and cost of capital? 
6 
A. 
The proportion and cost of debt capital available to utility companies are 
7 
influenced by the rating agencies’ assessment of the regulatory 
8 
environment.  Moody’s has highlighted the relevance of a stable and 
9 
predictable regulatory environment to a utility’s credit quality, stating that 
10 
“[b]roadly speaking, the Regulatory Framework is the foundation for how 
11 
all the decisions that affect utilities are made (including the setting of rates), 
12 
as well as the predictability and consistency of decision-making provided 
13 
by that foundation.”99 
14 
 
15

16

22

Q.

4

5 
A.

6

7 
corresponding pressure on credit metrics and, therefore, credit ratings.  A 
8 
2016 S&P report noted: 
9 
When applicable, a jurisdiction's willingness to support 
10 
large capital projects with cash during construction is an 
11 
important aspect of our analysis. This is especially true 
12 
when the project represents a major addition to rate base 
13 
and entails long lead times and technological risks that 
14 
make it susceptible to construction delays. Broad 
15 
support for all capital spending is the most credit-
16 
sustaining. Support for only specific types of capital 
17 
spending, such as specific environmental projects or 
18 
system integrity plans, is less so, but still favorable for 
19 
creditors. Allowance of a cash return on construction 
20 
work-in-progress or similar ratemaking methods 
21 
historically were extraordinary measures for use in 
22 
unusual circumstances, but when construction costs are 
23 
rising, cash flow support could be crucial to maintain 
24 
credit quality through the spending program. Even more 
25 
favorable are those jurisdictions that present an 
26 
opportunity for a higher return on capital projects as an 
27 
incentive to investors.103  
28 
                                                 
103 S&P Global Ratings, Ratings Direct, “Assessing U.S. Investor-Owned Utility 
Regulatory Environments,” August 10, 2016, at 7.

VIII. CAPITAL STRUCTURE 
Q.

1 
A.

2

5

6

7 
equity ratio for KEDLI for the same period ranges from 48.00 percent to 
8 
50.02 percent.105 The requested equity ratio is consistent with recent 
9 
Commission precedent regarding the authorized capital structure for

utilities.106

Q. 
What is the Commission’s policy on determining the authorized equity 
1 
ratio?   
2 
A. 
The Commission has allowed the use of a stand-alone equity ratio if a utility 
3 
can demonstrate that the credit rating agencies view that utility’s credit on 
4 
a stand-alone basis independent of its parent.107 
5 
 
6 
 Q. 
Do the credit rating agencies view KEDNY and KEDLI’s credit on a 
7 
stand-alone basis?   
8 
A. 
Yes, they do.  The credit rating agencies review and assess the credit risk 
9 
profile of the individual utility on a stand-alone basis, and both KEDNY 
10 
and KEDLI are rated on their own financial merits and business risk 
11 
profiles.  As noted previously, Moody’s recently downgraded the senior 
12 
unsecured and issuer ratings of KEDNY and KEDLI based on concerns 
13 
related to key credit metrics based on the Commission’s August 2018 order 
14 
to reduce revenue under the existing rate plan resulting from tax reform.  
15 
For KEDNY, Moody’s also indicated concerns for the additional 
16 
expenditures for environmental remediation and the expectation of 
17 
increased leverage to manage to the approved 48 percent equity ratio from 
18 
an actual equity ratio of 55.6 percent.108  
19 
 
20

Q. 
Please describe how the Moody’s reports for KEDNY and KEDLI 
1

2

3 
A.   
In recent reports, Moody’s notes that KEDLI and KEDNY both have strong 
4 
ring-fencing provisions that protect the stand-alone ratings. 
5 
While there is considerable debt at KEDLI’s parent 
6 
holding companies, the strong ring-fencing provisions 
7 
applicable to New York utilities reduce the potential for 
8 
debt to be pushed down to KEDLI. In particular, we 
9 
view the explicit leverage restriction for KEDLI (to 
10 
maintain debt /capitalisation ratio at less than 58%) as 
11 
providing the greatest credit support.  
12 
 
13 
Additional ring-fencing provisions imposed by the 
14 
NYPSC on KEDLI that we view as credit supportive 
15 
include: (1) the special preferred share provision that 
16 
reduces the probability of bankruptcy in a distressed 
17 
situation, and (2) the requirement for KEDLI to hold an 
18 
investment-grade rating. 109

22

24 
that the Commission has established for KEDNY and KEDLI and cite them 
25 
as the reason why they assess both Companies’ credit quality on a stand-
26 
alone basis.  Because there is factual evidence indicating that the two major 
27 
credit rating agencies view each of the Companies’ credit quality on a stand-
28

alone basis, the stand-alone capital structures proposed in this proceeding

12 
 
13 
Q. 
Have you conducted any analysis of the Companies’ proposed capital 
14 
structure as compared with the proxy companies? 
15 
A. 
Yes. I have reviewed KEDNY and KEDLI’s proposed capital structure as 
16 
compared with the actual capital structures of the operating companies in 
17 
the Combined Utility and Natural Gas Proxy Groups for the most recently 
18 
reported four years.  As shown on Exhibit __ (AEB-12), the mean annual 
19 
equity ratio of the proxy companies over that period is 56.65 percent for the 
20 
Combined Utility Group and 57.94 percent for the Natural Gas Proxy 
21 
Group.   
22 
 
23 


--- Page 101 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 99 of 103 
Q. 
What do you conclude from this analysis? 
1 
A. 
The requested 48 to 50 percent equity ratio is conservative considering the 
2 
equity ratios of the proxy companies and the current business and financial 
3 
risks of KEDNY and KEDLI, including significant capital investment 
4 
programs, credit metrics pressures and credit rating agency pressures.  This 
5 
information indicates that the utility operating subsidiaries owned by 
6 
holding companies with similar business characteristics to KEDNY and 
7 
KEDLI have for the last four years maintained average common equity 
8 
ratios more than 8.00 percentage points above the 48.0 percent equity ratio 
9 
that the Commission approved for KEDNY and KEDLI in the Companies’ 
10 
last rate proceeding.  These higher proxy equity ratios reflect a level of 
11 
financial risk that is lower than the financial risk implied by the proposed 
12 
48.0 percent equity ratio.  Therefore, I conclude that the requested equity 
13 
ratio should be considered a lower bound on the equity ratio relative to the 
14 
range of reasonableness established by industry practice.  As such, it would 
15 
be reasonable for the Commission to use higher equity ratios for KEDNY 
16 
and KEDLI closer to those of the proxy group operating companies for 
17 
ratemaking purposes. 
18 
 
19 
Q.  
Will the capital structure and ROE authorized in these proceedings 
20 
affect the Companies’ access to capital at reasonable rates? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  The level of earnings authorized by the Commission directly affects 
22 
the Companies’ ability to fund their operations with internally generated 
23 
funds.  Both bond investors and rating agencies expect a significant portion 
24 


--- Page 102 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 100 of 103 
of ongoing capital investments to be financed with internally generated 
1 
funds.  
2 
 
3 
It also is important to realize that because a utility’s investment horizon is 
4 
very long, investors require the assurance of a sufficiently high return to 
5 
satisfy the long-run financing requirements of the assets placed into service.  
6 
Those assurances, which often are measured by the relationship between 
7 
internally generated cash flows and debt (or interest expense), depend quite 
8 
heavily on the capital structure.  As a consequence, both the ROE and 
9 
capital structure are very important to debt and equity investors.  
10 
Furthermore, considering the capital market conditions discussed in Section 
11 
IV, the authorized ROE and capital structure take on even greater 
12 
significance.   
13 
 
14 
IX.   MULTI-YEAR RATE PLAN 
Q. 
Would a multi-year rate plan impact your ROE recommendation?   
15 
A.  
Yes, it would.  The Commission has in many cases approved three-year rate 
16 
case settlements that often include stay-out premiums.  It is my 
17 
understanding that the Companies will provide four years of forecast data 
18 
in their rate filings.  In keeping with Commission precedent, a stay-out 
19 
premium would reflect the increased risk faced by the Companies under a 
20 
multi-year rate plan. 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 103 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 101 of 103 
Q.  
How has New York typically estimated a stay-out premium?  
1 
A.  
The New York approach has typically set the measure of the risk and return 
2 
trade-off using one half of the yield spread between a one-year and three-
3 
year Treasury securities.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Does one half of the yield spread between one-year and three-year 
6 
Treasuries sufficiently reflect the risk to equity investors inherent in a 
7 
multi-year stay-out?  
8 
A. 
No.  The stay-out premium associated with a multi-year rate plan should not 
9 
only compensate investors for changes in the level of interest rates or 
10 
inflation, but also for the potential risk of under-earning that is introduced 
11 
by “staying out.”  By staying out of rate cases, the utility may not fully 
12 
recover material amounts of capital expenditures and may be required to 
13 
absorb losses due to differences between the cost of service established in 
14 
the rate plan and actual levels of revenue and expense.  The premium should 
15 
compensate the utility and its investors for these additional risks over and 
16 
above interest rate risk.  In the current market environment, there is 
17 
additional risk that the authorized ROE for the latter years of a multi-year 
18 
rate plan will be lower than investors’ future requirements as interest rates 
19 
are expected to increase.  
20 
 
21 


--- Page 104 ---
Testimony of Ann E. Bulkley 
Page 102 of 103 
Q. 
What do you propose as the stay-out premium for a four-year rate 
1 
plan?  
2 
A. 
The ROE proposed by the Companies of 9.65 percent will not provide the 
3 
Companies a return commensurate with the return available on investments 
4 
of similar risk over the term of the multi-year rate plan without an adequate 
5 
stay-out premium.  Consistent with prior cases in which a stay-out premium 
6 
was included in multi-year rate plans, I recommend that a stay-out premium 
7 
be included in a multi-year rate plan.  I believe 50 basis points is a 
8 
reasonable, albeit conservative, reflection of the incremental risk to the 
9 
Company under a multi-year stay-out provision.  
10 
  
11 
X.   CONCLUSION AND RECOMMENDATION 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding a fair return on book equity for 
12 
KEDNY and KEDLI? 
13 
A. 
My recommended return on book equity considers the results of the DCF 
14 
and CAPM models, summarized in Figure 14 (below), and the specific risks 
15 
to which the Companies are exposed.  I conclude that the appropriate ROE 
16 
for the Companies is within the range of 9.65 percent and 10.55 percent. 
17 
The Companies are requesting an ROE of 9.65 percent, which is at the low 
18 
end of the range of reasonableness and is a conservative estimate of the 
19 
investor-required ROE. Furthermore, if the Commission approves the stay-
20 
out period, a premium of 50 basis points should be added to the ROE.  
21 
 
22

Ann E. Bulkley 
Senior Vice President 
 
Ms. Bulkley has more than two decades of management and economic consulting experience in the 
energy industry.  Ms. Bulkley has extensive state and federal regulatory experience on both electric 
and natural gas issues including rate of return, cost of equity and capital structure issues. Ms. Bulkley 
has provided expert testimony on the cost of capital in more than 30 regulatory proceedings before 
regulatory commissions in Arizona, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, Kansas, Massachusetts, 
Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, North Dakota, Oklahoma, 
Pennsylvania, Texas, South Dakota, West Virginia, and the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.  
In addition, Ms. Bulkley has prepared and provided supporting analysis for at least forty Federal and 
State regulatory proceedings.  In addition, Ms. Bulkley has worked on acquisition teams with 
investors seeking to acquire utility assets, providing valuation services including an understanding 
of regulation, market expected returns, and the assessment of utility risk factors.  Ms. Bulkley has 
assisted clients with valuations of public utility and industrial properties for ratemaking, purchase 
and sale considerations, ad valorem tax assessments, and accounting and financial purposes.   In 
addition, Ms. Bulkley has experience in the areas of contract and business unit valuation, strategic 
alliances, market restructuring and regulatory and litigation support.  Prior to joining Concentric, Ms. 
Bulkley held senior expertise-based consulting positions at several firms, including Reed Consulting 
Group and Navigant Consulting, Inc. where she specialized in valuation.  Ms. Bulkley holds an M.A. in 
economics from Boston University and a B.A. in economics and finance from Simmons College.  Ms. 
Bulkley is a Certified General Appraiser licensed in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts and the 
State of New Hampshire. 
 
REPRESENTATIVE PROJECT EXPERIENCE 
Regulatory Analysis and Ratemaking 
Ms. Bulkley has provided a range of advisory services relating to regulatory policy analysis and many 
aspects of utility ratemaking.  Specific services have included: cost of capital and return on equity 
testimony, cost of service and rate design analysis and testimony, development of ratemaking 
strategies; development of merchant function exit strategies; analysis and program development to 
address residual energy supply and/or provider of last resort obligations; stranded costs assessment 
and recovery; performance-based ratemaking analysis and design; and many aspects of traditional 
utility ratemaking (e.g., rate design, rate base valuation).   
Cost of Capital  
Ms. Bulkley has provided expert testimony on the cost of capital in more than 30 regulatory 
proceedings before regulatory commissions in many states including Arizona, Arkansas,

Valuation 
Ms. Bulkley has provided valuation services to utility clients, unregulated generators and private 
equity clients for a variety of purposes including ratemaking, fair value, ad valorem tax, litigation 
and damages, and acquisition.  Ms. Bulkley’s appraisal practices are consistent with the national 
standards established by the Uniform Standards of Professional Appraisal Practice.  In addition, 
Ms. Bulkley has relied on other simulation based valuation methodologies.  
Representative projects/clients have included:

Northern Indiana Fuel and Light: Provided expert testimony regarding the fair value of 
the company’s natural gas distribution system assets. Valuation relied on cost approach.

gas distribution system assets. Valuation relied on cost approach.

several electric rate proceedings. Valuation approaches used in this project included 
income, cost and comparable sales approaches.

financing purposes for regulated utility client.  
•

used for strategic planning purposes.  Valuation approach included an income approach, 
a real options analysis and a risk analysis.  
•

underlying assets.  Performed analysis to determine the option value of a plant in a 
competitively priced electricity market following the settlement of the NUG contract. 
•

utilities in the sale of purchase power contracts.  Assignment included an assessment of 
the regional power market, analysis of the underlying purchase power contracts, a 
traditional discounted cash flow valuation approach, as well as a risk analysis.  Analyzed 
bids from potential acquirers using income and risk analysis approached.  Prepared an 
assessment of the credit issues and value at risk for the selling utility.

Prepared appraisal of a portfolio of generating facilities for a large electric utility to be 
used for financing purposes.  
• 
Prepared an appraisal of a fleet of fossil generating assets for a large electric utility to 
establish the value of assets transferred from utility property. 
• 
Conducted due diligence on an electric transmission and distribution system as part of a 
buy-side due diligence team.  
• 
Provided analytical support for and prepared appraisal reports of generation assets to be 
used in ad valorem tax disputes.

11945134 
Quantitative Profiles 
Where are we in the cycle?
12 December 2018 
 
Mid, late, end? 
Factor rotation based on economic cycle characteristics has historically been fairly well 
behaved – in broad strokes, deep value/”junk” has worked well during early cycle 
markets, earnings and price momentum in mid-cycle, quality and large caps in late cycle 
and low risk at the end of a cycle. But this framework has been particularly challenging, 
as is evidenced in two exhibits: our US Regime indicator (Chart 1, below) which has been 
bouncing around between mid and late for the last six months and recently moved into 
late cycle, and our economics team’s model on the phase of the business cycle according 
to bid data (see Chart 2 on p.2) has suggested we have been in a 9-year soft-patch, an 
unusual anomaly relative to better behaved economic cycles. Our US Regime indicator 
today would argue for High Quality leadership, where our work on the VIX corroborates 
this call. For those concerned with an impending bear market, as we note in our Year 
Ahead, the best performing factors in bear markets have been Low Beta and High 
Dividend yield. For those expecting a violent snap back, deep value and high risk could 
be more fruitful. We would stick with high quality (B+ or Better) stocks (more below) for 
the time being. 
Positioning generated 8ppt alpha in November 
After a +7% decline in October, the eq-wtd S&P 500 index rebounded by 2.5% in 
November, but a “risk-off” mood prevailed. Factor leadership remained narrow, with two 
of every three factors underperforming the benchmark. The traditional “risk-on” factor 
groups (High Risk, Growth, and Momentum) remained the laggards (more below). High 
Dividend Yield, which tends to lead in declining markets, outperformed (+3.3% in Nov. / 
+5.0% YTD), and return-based quality factors (High ROE, ROA, ROC) were mixed, with
half of them outperforming. Notably and in keeping with our work on rising volatility,
stocks with B+ or Better S&P quality ranks (i.e., high quality) outperformed “B or Worse” 
rated stocks (i.e., low quality) by ~2ppt. Neglected stocks led in November, as
Institutional Neglect (+3.1% Nov. / +1.4% YTD) and Low Analyst Coverage (+4.2% Nov., 
best overall / +0.4% YTD) fared well and suggests that the selloff has been driven by
investors de-grossing, or taking down market exposure. The 10 stocks least owned by
active managers in our mutual fund holdings report once again beat the 10 most owned
stocks by a very wide (+8.4ppt) margin last month.
Screens and performance data are available in Research Library in Excel format

BofA Merrill Lynch does and seeks to do business with issuers covered in its research reports.

11951407 
 
  
  
 
 
 
Quantitative Profiles 
  
 
Quality led in 2018; favor Quality, Large 
Cap and FCF in 2019 
 
 
 
11 January 2019   
 
Worst December on record; worst year since crisis 
The selloff, which started in October, accelerated last month with the equal-weighted 
S&P 500 declining 10% (the worst December on our record since ‘86), which resulted in 
the worst year (-9.5%) since financial crisis. All of the factors we follow finished the year 
in the red, but Quality factors (-5.8%) were the best performers amid the volatile market. 
Momentum factors (-7.2%) were the second-best performers, and Growth (-7.9%) factors 
led Value (-13.7%) factors. Risk (-15.3%) was the single worst performing group amid a 
risk-off year. Factor breadth was the worst since financial crisis - 38% of the 
fundamental quantitative factors we track (excluding Momentum) beat the index in ‘18. 
Low beta in 2018, high quality in 2019 
High beta stocks were the worst performing decile of the S&P 500, and beta saw the 
widest return spread of the factors we track (+18ppt, based on top-bottom decile). Today, 
high beta stocks trade at a near record discount to low beta stocks. We expect a reversal in 
this trend. High quality stocks by EPS rather than price volatility also led We expect this to 
persist. High quality now trades at a slight premium to low quality, and we think high 
quality might re-rate higher. But high quality<>low beta, and therein lies the opportunity. 
Earnings surprises and revisions tended to matter in 2018 
Earnings trends remained in focus in 2018 - stocks with the highest EPS estimate 
revisions outperformed the index by nearly 4ppt last year, and stocks with the most 
positive EPS surprises led by nearly 2ppt. Earnings trends are likely to remain in focus as 
earnings growth is slated to moderate from +23% in 2018 to +5% in 2019. 
From mid to late cycle 
Since the start of 2017, our US Regime indicator has suggested we are in mid-cycle – a 
phase consistent with the outperformance of Momentum and Growth. But in 4Q18, the 
indicator shifted into late cycle. What works in late cycle? High quality and low-risk 
stocks, as well as large caps over small caps. We see risk to momentum strategies in ‘19 
(more below.) We believe 2019 will boil down to buying sources of cash and selling uses 
of cash. Free cash flow (FCF) is king, and we favor FCF based valuation measures over 
EPS or sales based measures in late cycle. We also prefer medium-duration stocks 
(dividend growth stocks with room to raise payouts) over long and short duration names. 
Biggest risk in 2019? Momentum 
Momentum factors outperformed in 2019 and helped active managers add alpha in 2018. 
But Momentum exposure could be a risk in 2019: our “Rule of 25” suggests that 
momentum strategies typically outperform when the VIX is <25 (VIX averaged 17 last 
year), but lag when the VIX is >25 (where it is today). And a flattening yield curve suggests 
higher volatility over the next three years. 
Screens and performance data are available in Research Library in Excel format 
Chart 1: Style performance in 2018 
Source:  BofA Merrill Lynch US Equity & Quant Strategy

Quantitative Profiles 
  
 
January reversal was a head-fake; Regime 
model says double down on large quality 
 
 
 
08 February 2019   
 
Moving deeper into late cycle; buy large cap high quality 
The last move in our US Regime indicator was deeper into a late cycle market 
environment which is typically (although not always) followed by the recession phase. 
(Chart 2 inside). Historically, when the regime model has fallen to these levels, the next 
phase has been recession 75% of the time, which occurred 2.7 months later, on avg. The 
styles that have historically outperformed the most during both late cycle and recession 
phases are Quality, Low Risk and Large Caps. While mega-caps, Low Risk and Quality 
were among the laggards last month, they may prove to be the best hedges if our 
regime indicator further deteriorates. (MTD, Quality and Large Size have outperformed.) 
Reversals break Momentum - January was no exception 
Momentum factors (+6.8% on avg.), which were the second best performing group in 
2018, benefited least from the January rally –all of the price momentum factors that we 
follow lagged the index and the worst five factors in January were all Momentum 
measures, with +4.4% to +5.5% returns. Accordingly, “anti-momentum”, or reversals, 
fared well, as this group gained 14.6%. Momentum exposure could be at risk in 2019: as 
it tends to lag during periods of heightened volatility, where the flattening yield curve 
portends an extended period of rising volatility. 
A risk-on and deep value January 
As risk assets took their cue from oversold levels and a more dovish Fed, they rushed to 
recover much of 2018 losses, resulting in one of the strongest monthly returns for equities 
– the equal weighted S&P 500 index gained 9.9%, in the top 10 best monthly returns in 
our history since ’86. Factor groups which suffered most last year, Risk and Value, 
outperformed other groups in January: Risk gained +13.8%, more than other factor groups, 
while Value was 2nd best group with a +12.0% return. High Beta (+15.8%) gained most 
among Risk factors and Low Fwd P/E (+14.6%) led Value. 
Quality ailed in January, but likely to resume a lead in ‘19 
Quality, the 2018 winner, also fell victim to the sharp January reversal – this group was 
2nd weakest last month with a +8.9% gain. Only High ROA (+10.1%) stocks cleared the 
index last month, while other quality factors lagged with +7.6% to 9.7% returns. For the 
whole year, high quality is likely to lead, as quality has historically been a beneficiary of 
rising volatility and tightening monetary policy. 
Growth stocks uptrend 
Growth stocks lost ground in 4Q18, but resumed their uptrend in January, and Growth was 
the only 2018 leader that also outperformed the index last month (+11.0% on avg.). High 
Projected 5-yr EPS Growth factor fared best (+14.1%, among the top five factors overall), 
while Long Duration (+9.1%) gained the least. 
Screens and performance data are available in Research Library in Excel format 
Chart 1:  2018 losers bloomed amid January reversal 
Source: BofA Merrill Lynch US Equity and US Quant Strategy
